# Entire final proof

# P02.3: weakly smooth decreasing regularization, singular curves, and an analytic-boundary obstruction

This is an unverified research draft. The full characterization for arbitrary classical psh germs on higher-dimensional good strict analytic spaces remains open. The results below are sufficient sectors and a sharp obstruction within a particular boundary family. Successful source checks and TeX builds are not mathematical verification.

## 1. Conventions and checked published premises

An analytic extension means a complete isometric extension of the ground field. All neighborhoods and overlaps in our conclusions are ordinary open sets. Classical psh means GR, Definition 4.1: an upper semicontinuous function with values in \(\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}\) whose pullback to every analytic curve after every analytic extension is subharmonic. Identically minus infinity is permitted. For smooth connected strict curves over a nontrivially valued field, GR 3.4.1 identifies this class with Thuillier's subharmonic functions together with the constant minus infinity. No continuity or finite-value hypothesis is imposed on the starting function.

We use the following checked primary results. References refer to the cached versions, not to an unpublished book.

GR denotes Gubler--Rabinoff, arXiv:2506.13548v2. Proposition 3.5(3) says that an upper semicontinuous function on a curve which is subharmonic on its analytic interior is subharmonic on the whole curve. Proposition 4.2 gives ordinary sheafiness, positive linear combinations and maxima, decreasing nets, scalar extension in both directions, pullback, and descent along finite surjective morphisms. Theorem 6.1 gives the local maximum principle at analytic interior points. Theorem 6.5 gives stability under a pointwise convergent net when its limit is upper semicontinuous. Lemma 3.9 describes finite normalization of reduced strict curves: over a perfect field it is rig-smooth, is an isomorphism outside discrete rigid points, is a quotient, and locally splits into finitely many components, each meeting exactly one normalization preimage of the singular point. Its proof also records local constancy of invertible analytic absolute values at rigid points.

GJR denotes Gubler--Jell--Rabinoff, arXiv:2111.05741. Its standing field is complete and nontrivially valued and its spaces are good and strictly analytic. Definition 5.1 uses the G-topology generated by strict affinoid domains. Definition 8.1 defines harmonic tropicalizations using integer-PL harmonic coordinates. Proposition 7.5 expresses real harmonic germs as real linear combinations of integer harmonic germs. Proposition 7.6 and Remark 8.2(4) give pullback and arbitrary scalar extension of harmonic coordinates. Definitions 10.3 and 10.7 define weakly smooth functions by smooth Lagerberg functions pulled back along harmonic tropicalizations, with sheafification in the ordinary topology; 10.8 gives pullback. Proposition 10.9 describes common-chart equality. Remark 10.10 includes ordinary CLD forms in weakly smooth forms. Proposition 14.2 gives finite Galois descent for weakly smooth forms. Proposition 14.5(1) states equality of integrals under finite Galois scalar extension, without a degree factor. Sections 11 and 12 provide integration and strong currents on Hausdorff boundaryless pure-dimensional spaces, with Stokes and \(d'd''[v]=[d'd''v]\) for weakly smooth functions.

GJR Proposition 15.7 identifies real harmonic functions on rig-smooth curves with Thuillier harmonic functions. Proposition 15.9 concerns piecewise \((\mathbb Z,\Gamma)\)-linear harmonic skeleton functions on the indicated strict subgraphs. It is not a statement about arbitrary real bends on arbitrary domains. Remark 15.10 allows harmonic functions which are weakly smooth but not ordinary CLD smooth. **Proposition 15.11 states a decreasing net**, on each relatively compact ordinary open subset of a smooth boundaryless curve, for arbitrary Thuillier-subharmonic targets. The proof's last sentence extracts a sequence for an individual piecewise real-linear target; it does not replace the preceding arbitrary-target net by a sequence.

Thuillier, thesis tel-00010990v1, distinguishes the all-domain site \(X_G\) from the strict rigid site \(X_R\) in 2.1.1. Definition 3.2.1 of lisse uses all-domain affinoid coverings. Theorem 3.4.2 and its proof give a decreasing family indexed by finite subsets, not a sequence. We do not assert strict GJR real-PL membership for every such lisse function.

Wanner, arXiv:1801.04713v3, assumes an algebraically closed complete nontrivially valued field. Corollary 5.4 assumes a smooth proper algebraic curve, residue field algebraic over a finite field or a projective-line/Mumford-curve alternative, and a finite continuous current-psh target on an open subset. Its compact Dini argument concerns that continuous target. It is not an arbitrary classical-potential theorem. Only the elementary regularized-maximum mechanism in its Lemma 5.2 and the proof of GJR 15.11 is used here.

CD denotes the actual saved arXiv:1204.6277v2. Its relevant numbering is Definition 8.1.1, Lemma 8.1.4, Proposition 8.1.5, and Proposition 8.3.1, for ordinary smooth psh generators, transfinite stages, pullbacks and current positivity. Its analytic ordinary-smooth description is Corollary 15.1.2; Lemma 1.2.11 gives linear pullback of positivity. These statements do not identify all classical functions with the first smooth stage, or replace harmonic tropicalizations by analytic moments. The other GR source, arXiv:2503.05513, concerns strict real-PL semipositivity and its curve comparison; it is not used to infer weak smoothness of arbitrary lisse functions. Berkovich 1993, Proposition 1.5.5, is the boundary-composition premise as restated in GR 2.11.

## 2. Auxiliary arguments, including smoothness and positivity

### 2.1. Countable extraction with extended-real limits

Let \(Z\) be second countable and let \((a_i)_{i\in I}\) be a directed decreasing net of finite continuous real functions with pointwise infimum \(a:Z\to\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}\). Then there are indices \(i_j\le i_{j+1}\) with \(a_{i_j}\downarrow a\) at every point.

For each rational \(q\), the open sets \(\{a_i<q\}\) cover the open set \(\{a<q\}\). Every open subspace of a second-countable space is Lindelof: given a cover, select for each member of a countable base contained in one cover member one such member. Thus countably many indices suffice for each rational \(q\). Enumerate all selected indices and take successively directed upper bounds. If \(a(z)\) is finite, every rational \(q>a(z)\) eventually bounds the selected values at \(z\); if \(a(z)=-\infty\), every rational does. The reverse inequality follows from \(a_i\ge a\). This is extraction by values, not a claim that the original index set has a countable cofinal subset.

If \(K\) has a countable dense subset, each strict affinoid algebra is separable in its Banach norm. Indeed, polynomials in finitely many variables with coefficients from a countable dense subfield are dense in a Tate algebra, and their images are dense in a quotient. For a norm-dense sequence \((b_n)\), evaluations \(x\mapsto |b_n(x)|\) generate the Berkovich topology: the inequality
\[
\big||b(x)|-|c(x)|\big|\le |(b-c)(x)|\le\|b-c\|
\]
gives uniform approximation of every evaluation. The countable evaluations separate points and embed the compact spectrum into a countable product of bounded real intervals. Hence the spectrum, and every ordinary open subset of it, is second countable. This proof is local and does not require global countability of an analytic space.

### 2.2. Compact domination without continuity of the target

If \(a_i\downarrow a\), the \(a_i\) are continuous, \(A\) is compact, and a finite continuous \(b\) satisfies \(b>a\) on \(A\), then some index satisfies \(a_i<b\) on all of \(A\). The open sets \(\{a_i<b\}\) cover \(A\). A finite subcover and one directed upper bound give the conclusion. Additional finitely many index or point-value requirements can be imposed by taking another upper bound. This argument works when \(a\) is discontinuous or minus infinity and is not Dini's theorem.

### 2.3. A regularized maximum and classical positivity

Choose an even nonnegative smooth probability density \(\rho\) supported in \((-1,1)\). For \(\delta>0\), set
\[
\theta_\delta(t)=\int_{-1}^1 |t+\delta s|\rho(s)\,ds,
\qquad M_\delta(a,b)=\frac{a+b+\theta_\delta(a-b)}2.
\]
Convolution makes \(\theta_\delta\) smooth and convex. Its derivative lies in \([-1,1]\). Jensen's inequality and the triangle inequality imply
\[
\max(a,b)\le M_\delta(a,b)\le\max(a,b)+\delta/2.
\]
It equals the maximum when \(|a-b|\ge\delta\), and is convex and nondecreasing in each variable. These facts prove all bounds used below.

If \(g_1,\ldots,g_r\) are finite continuous classical psh functions and \(F\) is a finite smooth convex function on an open convex region containing their values, nondecreasing in each variable, then \(F(g_1,\ldots,g_r)\) is classically psh. Supporting affine functions to \(F\) have nonnegative coefficients. Their finite maxima evaluated on the \(g_i\) are classically psh by GR 4.2. The directed net of finite maxima converges pointwise to the finite continuous composition. GR Theorem 6.5 applies. The argument also applies to a convex nondecreasing function of \(\log|P|\) which is constant on a negative half-line: its constant supporting line controls zeros, so the composition remains finite and continuous there. This proves classical positivity of every regularized maximum used below; it does not follow from its smoothness label.

### 2.4. What the curve approximants actually look like

We use GJR 15.11 as the published regularization premise. Its proof has a more precise local description which is needed for pullbacks and current positivity. After a finite Galois extension, an individual lisse target is represented on a finite skeleton. At a vertex with outgoing slopes \(s_e\) and weights \(w_e>0\), put
\[
\mu=\sum_e w_es_e>0,\qquad
g_e=s_e-\frac{\mu}{\sum_e w_e}.
\]
The star function with value \(F(x)\) and outgoing slopes \(g_e\) is harmonic, lies strictly below \(F\) off \(x\), and agrees with it at \(x\). A sufficiently small \(M_\delta(G+\epsilon,F)\), with \(\delta<\epsilon\), equals \(G+\epsilon\) near the vertex and equals \(F\) near the outer ends. These are equalities on ordinary neighborhoods, so the patched functions agree on the ordinary overlaps. Arbitrary real subdivisions in this argument are not asserted to come from strict formal models.

Off these small stars the function is harmonic. Near a vertex it is harmonic plus a constant. On each remaining annulus it is a smooth convex function \(\Phi(t)\) of an invertible logarithmic annulus coordinate \(t\). Harmonic regions are weakly smooth because GJR 15.7 and 7.5 write their real harmonic potential as a smooth affine function of finitely many integer harmonic coordinates. Annular regions are weakly smooth using the smooth tropicalization \(t\). The finite Galois construction can be made invariant by averaging its finitely many translates: averages retain weak smoothness, classical positivity, approximation bounds and decreasing order. Proposition 14.2 then descends the weakly smooth functions. The harmonic pieces are subharmonic, annular convex pieces are subharmonic, and ordinary sheafiness gives classical positivity, using GR 3.4.1 and 4.1.2. The identically minus-infinity component is handled separately by constants \(-j\).

For clarity, the local curvature check is explicit. Harmonic pieces have \(d'd''g=0\). Annular pieces have
\[
d'd''g=\Phi''(t)\,d't\wedge d''t,\qquad\Phi''\ge0.
\]
Convex compositions and regularized maxima have positive semidefinite Hessians in these harmonic coordinates. Under an analytic morphism, each integer harmonic coordinate pulls back to an integer harmonic coordinate by GJR 7.6, so the same formulas hold with \(t\) or the harmonic tuple composed with the morphism. A real harmonic coordinate is represented by an affine function of an integer harmonic tuple; its Hessian is zero. Thus these pullbacks are weakly smooth with positive curvature, rather than merely being assigned a smoothness name.

Here is the current interpretation whenever the target is Hausdorff, boundaryless, good strict, and pure of dimension \(d\). A positive semidefinite Hessian gives a strongly positive \((1,1)\)-form: diagonalize the matrix to obtain a sum of nonnegative multiples of \(\ell\wedge J\ell\). Wedge with a positive compactly supported test form of bidegree \((d-1,d-1)\); the Lagerberg positivity cones in that degree coincide by CD 1.2.10, so it is enough to use strongly positive tests. On a common harmonic tropical chart the resulting top-degree coefficients are nonnegative. Integration uses the nonnegative tropical multiplicities of GJR and is nonnegative. Localization over the compact support proves
\[
\langle d'd''[g],\eta\rangle=\int d'd''g\wedge\eta\ge0.
\]
Stokes in GJR 11--12 justifies the equality. If the formula was obtained only after a finite Galois extension, pull back \(\eta\) and use GJR 14.5(1): the integrals are equal, with no degree factor. Finite averaging is harmless. This proves positive strong curvature currents and hence positive CLD currents by restricting the test space. It is a proof of positivity for the constructed functions, not a general equivalence between classical and current positivity for all weakly smooth functions.

These facts remain true after every analytic extension \(L/K\). The coordinates pull back by GJR 8.2(4). A finite Galois auxiliary extension can be composited with \(L\), giving a finite Galois extension of \(L\); the same integral argument applies. Classical positivity also follows independently from GR 4.2(5). No extraction of a new sequence over \(L\) is needed for the pullback of a sequence already constructed over \(K\).

## 3. A sequence theorem for singular curves, including several branches

**Theorem A.** Let \(K\) be complete, perfect, nontrivially valued, with a countable dense subset. Let \(C\) be a reduced, Hausdorff, good, strictly \(K\)-analytic curve with empty analytic boundary. For every ordinary open \(D\subset C\), every classical psh \(u:D\to\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}\), and every \(x\in D\), there is one ordinary open neighborhood \(V\subset D\) of \(x\) and a sequence of finite weakly smooth functions \(v_j\) on \(V\) such that \(v_j\) is classically psh, \(d'd''[v_j]\) is a positive strong current, and \(v_j\downarrow u|_V\) pointwise. The same sequence pulls back with these properties after every analytic extension. If \(u\) is finite continuous, the convergence is locally uniform. This theorem permits singularities with several branches, as well as unibranch singularities. No residue-field algebraicity or Mumford hypothesis is used.

**Proof.** On the nonsingular locus, normalization is an isomorphism and is rig-smooth over the perfect field. Shrink to a relatively compact ordinary neighborhood in a smooth normalization component, apply GJR 15.11, handle a minus-infinity component by constants, and use 2.1. Its positivity and pullbacks are covered by 2.4.

Consider a singular point \(x\), necessarily rigid. By GR Lemma 3.9, shrink a connected neighborhood so that it has no other singularity and the finite normalization
\[
\nu:\coprod_{i=1}^r U_i\longrightarrow U
\]
has one preimage \(x_i\) of \(x\) on each connected smooth branch \(U_i\), and is an isomorphism away from these points. Choose the \(U_i\) relatively compact in slightly larger smooth neighborhoods on which \(u\circ\nu\) is defined, and inside a normalized strict affinoid neighborhood. These are second countable by 2.1. Such a choice is possible by finiteness, ordinary local compactness, and the local description in GR 3.9. Choose a compact strict affinoid neighborhood \(Y\subset U\) of \(x\), whose ordinary interior \(V\) contains \(x\). Let
\[
A_i=\nu^{-1}(Y)\cap U_i,\qquad A=\coprod_i A_i.
\]
These are compact. Write \(u_i=u\circ\nu|_{U_i}\) and \(a=u(x)=u_i(x_i)\). On each branch, GJR and 2.1 supply a decreasing sequence \(s_{i,j}\) of finite weakly smooth classical psh functions, with the curvature descriptions of 2.4, converging pointwise to \(u_i\). If \(u_i\equiv-\infty\), use \(s_{i,j}=-j\).

Every weakly smooth function is locally constant at a rigid point of a curve. Indeed, its local preform uses finitely many integer harmonic coordinates. Each is strict PL. At a rigid point a strict affinoid G-cover has a member containing an ordinary neighborhood of that point, as recorded in the proof of GR 3.9(6). Invertible analytic absolute values are locally constant there. Each coordinate, and therefore the smooth function of them, is locally constant. In the singular descent below we only use this assertion on the smooth normalization branches.

Take \(\epsilon_0=1\) and \(\epsilon_j=8^{-j}\). Upper semicontinuity bounds \(u_i\) above on the compact closures of the \(U_i\); choose one constant \(h_{i,0}=H\) for all branches with \(H\ge u_i+1\). We construct functions \(h_{i,j}\) on the fixed \(U_i\), satisfying \(h_{i,j}\ge u_i+\epsilon_j\), which decrease on \(A_i\) and have exactly the same value at all \(x_i\).

Suppose first that \(a\) is finite. Choose a sufficiently late term \(g_i\) of the branch sequence so that
\[
g_i\le s_{i,j},\qquad g_i<h_{i,j-1}-4\epsilon_j\ \text{on }A_i,
\qquad a\le b_i:=g_i(x_i)<a+\epsilon_j.
\]
The compact inequality is available by 2.2 because \(h_{i,j-1}\ge u_i+\epsilon_{j-1}\) and \(4\epsilon_j<\epsilon_{j-1}\). The point-value inequality follows from convergence. Put
\[
b=\max_i b_i,\qquad h_{i,j}=g_i+(b-b_i)+\epsilon_j.
\]
Then \(0\le b-b_i<\epsilon_j\), all \(h_{i,j}(x_i)=b+\epsilon_j\), and
\[
u_i+\epsilon_j\le h_{i,j}\le s_{i,j}+2\epsilon_j,
\qquad h_{i,j}<h_{i,j-1}-2\epsilon_j\ \text{on }A_i.
\]
Addition of constants preserves the smoothness and both positivity assertions. The common value and local constancy give equality on full neighborhoods of the normalization preimages, not just at isolated points.

Suppose next that \(a=-\infty\). Choose a finite number
\[
c_j\le-j,\qquad c_j<\min_A h_{j-1}-4\epsilon_j,
\]
where \(h_{j-1}\) denotes the continuous function on the disjoint union of branches. Choose a late \(g_i\) with
\[
g_i\le s_{i,j},\quad g_i<h_{i,j-1}-4\epsilon_j\ \text{on }A_i,
\quad g_i(x_i)<c_j-\epsilon_j.
\]
Define
\[
h_{i,j}=M_{\epsilon_j}(g_i,c_j)+\epsilon_j.
\]
It equals \(c_j+\epsilon_j\) near every \(x_i\). Section 2.3 proves classical positivity; the convex harmonic-chart formulas in 2.4 prove weak smoothness and current positivity. The bounds are
\[
u_i+\epsilon_j\le h_{i,j}\le\max(s_{i,j},c_j)+3\epsilon_j/2,
\qquad h_{i,j}<h_{i,j-1}-5\epsilon_j/2\ \text{on }A_i.
\]
They also cover branches identically minus infinity. Since \(c_j\to-\infty\), they prove pointwise convergence to \(u_i\).

In either case, the branch functions descend to finite continuous functions \(v_j\) on \(V\). To check ordinary smoothness-class descent, away from \(x\) use the normalization isomorphism. Near \(x\), choose the open neighborhoods on which every \(h_{i,j}\) has the common constant value. The finite normalization is closed and a quotient; the complement of the image of the complementary closed set gives an ordinary neighborhood of \(x\) on which \(v_j\) is constant. Thus \(v_j\) is weakly smooth in an actual neighborhood of the singularity and all chart overlaps agree. Finite surjective classical descent, GR 4.2(6), proves that \(v_j\) is classically psh. Alternatively, it is constant at the singularity and classically psh on the normalization-isomorphic complement. The curvature is zero on that constant neighborhood and has the positive formulas of 2.4 elsewhere, proving positive strong currents on \(V\).

The inequalities on the fixed compact \(A\) imply decrease on the fixed ordinary \(V\); the pointwise bounds imply its limit is \(u\), including its minus-infinity values. Section 2.4 proves scalar compatibility. On every compact subset where the target is finite continuous, ordinary Dini for this sequence gives uniform convergence. No uniform convergence is claimed for arbitrary singular targets. This proves the theorem.

The cusp \(y^2=x^3\) over a characteristic-zero field as in the theorem is a concrete unibranch example. A reduced nodal curve illustrates why independently choosing normalization approximants is insufficient: their values over the node must be aligned. The finite-branch construction above performs that alignment on one fixed neighborhood. It does not assert general weak-smooth descent along an arbitrary finite morphism.

## 4. Noninvariant higher-dimensional sector on saturated proper-fibre domains

**Theorem B.** Let \(K\) be as in Theorem A, and let \(C\) be a curve as there. Let \(f:X\to C\) be a proper surjective morphism with geometrically connected nonempty analytic fibres, where \(X\) is Hausdorff, good, strictly analytic, and pure of dimension \(d\ge2\). Suppose \(f\) has analytic sections locally on an ordinary open cover of \(C\). For every ordinary open \(D\subset C\) and every arbitrary classical psh function \(u\) on the **saturated domain** \(f^{-1}(D)\), every \(x\in f^{-1}(D)\) has a fixed ordinary open neighborhood \(f^{-1}(V)\), with \(f(x)\in V\subset D\), and finite weakly smooth functions
\[
w_j=v_j\circ f\quad\text{on }f^{-1}(V)
\]
which are classically psh, have positive strong curvature currents, and decrease pointwise to \(u\). For finite continuous \(u\), convergence is locally uniform. The same sequence works by pullback after every analytic extension. Fibres need not be smooth or normal.

**Proof of fibre constancy.** For \(c\in D\), pass to \(\mathcal H(c)\). GR scalar stability and pullback show that the restriction of \(u\) to the proper connected fibre \(X_c\) is classically psh. The fibre is compact and has empty analytic boundary. If it is not identically minus infinity, upper semicontinuity gives a finite attained maximum \(M\). The maximum set \(\{u=M\}\) is nonempty and closed. GR Theorem 6.1 makes it open, because every fibre point is an analytic interior point. Connectedness makes it the whole fibre. If the function is identically minus infinity, constancy already holds. Thus \(u\) has one value on each fibre, including fibres over nonrigid base points. This argument does not use density of rational points.

There is a unique function \(a:D\to\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}\) with \(u=a\circ f\). On an ordinary open admitting a section \(s\), \(a=u\circ s\), so it is upper semicontinuous and classically psh by GR pullback. On overlaps these functions are equal by fibre constancy, so ordinary sheafiness gives a classical psh function on \(D\). Apply Theorem A to \(a\) at \(f(x)\) and pull back its sequence.

The explicit local preforms are \((h\circ f,\alpha)\) for a harmonic chart \((h,\alpha)\) for \(v_j\). GJR 7.6 makes the coordinates harmonic. The common-chart equality on overlaps follows by pulling back the joint chart \((h,h')\), as in Definitions 10.3 and Proposition 10.9. The positive Hessian/zero-curvature representations, including the constant neighborhoods over singular base points, pull back exactly as in 2.4. Hence the strong currents are positive. Since \(f\) is proper and \(C\) has empty boundary, GR 2.11 implies \(X\) and \(f^{-1}(V)\) have empty analytic boundary, so these currents are defined without boundary residues.

For any analytic \(L/K\), let \(p:X_L\to X\). On the one fixed ordinary domain \(p^{-1}f^{-1}(V)=f_L^{-1}(V_L)\), the sequence is \(w_j\circ p\). It remains decreasing, and its value at a point \(z\) tends to \(u(p(z))\). Harmonic pullback, the scalar positivity argument in 2.4, and GR 4.2(5) prove all other assertions. This constructs one base-field sequence and then extends it; it does not attempt descent of arbitrarily chosen extension-field approximants. If \(u\) is finite continuous, a local section makes \(a\) finite continuous; compact images under \(f\) and the local uniform convergence of \(v_j\) give local uniform convergence of \(w_j\).

Examples include \(X=F^{\mathrm{an}}\times C\) for a geometrically connected proper algebraic \(F\) with a \(K\)-point, provided the displayed space is pure-dimensional. The point gives a section and projectivity supplies actual examples such as \(F=\mathbb P^m\), or a geometrically connected reduced nodal projective curve. The latter gives a singular surface when \(C\) is a curve. For \(C=\mathbb A^{1,\mathrm{an}}\), the function \(\log|T-a|\) pulled back from the base, with \(a\ne0\), is an arbitrary-class example with a genuine pole; on a torus open its base potential is not generally maximal-compact-torus invariant. The theorem assumes no torus invariance of any starting function.

**The domain quantifier matters.** Theorem B regularizes every classical psh function on each saturated domain \(f^{-1}(D)\). It does not regularize every sheaf germ on every ordinary open subset of \(X\). For instance, on an affine chart of \(\mathbb P^m\times C\), \(\log|Z_1|\) is classical psh and varies along the affine fibres; the compact-fibre argument does not apply there. Properness of \(X\) or of its fibres therefore cannot be advertised as a characterization of the original local sheaf property.

## 5. A sharp analytic-boundary family

**Theorem C.** For a complete nontrivially valued \(K\), let
\[
E=\mathcal M(K\langle T\rangle),\qquad \xi=\text{its radius-one Gauss point},\qquad
b(\xi)=1,\quad b(z)=0\ (z\ne\xi).
\]
This is an upper semicontinuous classical psh function on the good strict rig-smooth closed disc. If \(\widetilde K\) is uncountable, on no ordinary neighborhood of \(\xi\) is \(b\) a pointwise limit of a decreasing sequence of finite continuous functions; indeed, the argument below excludes any pointwise convergent sequence of continuous functions. If \(\widetilde K\) is countable, there is an explicit decreasing sequence of finite weakly smooth classical psh functions on the whole fixed closed disc converging to \(b\), with positive curvature on its analytic interior. Thus residue countability is an exact threshold for this particular boundary-spike function, not a characterization for all psh functions on discs.

**Classical membership and the actual boundary.** The analytic boundary of \(E\) is \(\{\xi\}\). The function is upper semicontinuous since its strict sublevel set for \(0<a\le1\) is \(E\setminus\{\xi\}\). It is constant zero on \(\operatorname{Int}(E)\). GR Proposition 3.5(3) makes it subharmonic on \(E\), and GR 4.1.2 makes it classically psh. This supplies, in particular, the required tests after every analytic extension. It is not an interior spike on a boundaryless space.

**Uncountable-residue obstruction.** Every ordinary neighborhood \(N\) of \(\xi\) contains the rational points in all but finitely many residue classes of the unit disc. To prove this, choose a basic neighborhood specified by finitely many polynomial absolute-value conditions. Such conditions generate the spectrum topology: analytic functions are uniformly approximated by polynomials, and their norms at \(\xi\) are nonzero unless the function is zero. Normalize each nonzero polynomial by a coefficient of maximum absolute value. Its reduction is a nonzero polynomial in \(\widetilde K[T]\), which has only finitely many roots in \(\widetilde K\). For a lift \(a\in K^\circ\) of a residue class avoiding all these roots, each polynomial has precisely its Gauss absolute value at \(a\); hence \(a\in N\).

Suppose continuous functions \(q_j\) on a neighborhood \(N\) converge pointwise to \(b\). Since \(q_j(\xi)\to1\), discard finitely many terms to have \(q_j(\xi)>3/4\). Each \(N_j=\{q_j>3/4\}\cap N\) is an ordinary neighborhood of \(\xi\). Each excludes rational points from only finitely many residue classes by the preceding argument. Choose a residue class outside the countable union of exceptions, and one lift \(a\). Then \(q_j(a)>3/4\) for all remaining \(j\), contrary to \(b(a)=0\). Weakly smooth functions are continuous, so this is a fortiori a failure of the requested sequential regularization. It does not exclude decreasing nets.

**Countable-residue construction.** Enumerate the monic irreducible polynomials of \(\widetilde K[T]\) as \(p_1,p_2,\ldots\), and choose coefficientwise lifts \(P_i\in K^\circ[T]\) which are monic and have Gauss norm one. Choose a nonnegative smooth probability density \(\sigma\) supported in \((0,1)\), symmetric about \(1/2\). Set
\[
\eta(t)=2\int_t^1\sigma(s)\,ds,\qquad
\psi(t)=\int_0^t\eta(s)\,ds,
\]
where \(\sigma\) is zero outside \((0,1)\). Then \(\eta\) is smooth, nonnegative and nonincreasing; it is \(2\) for \(t\le0\) and zero for \(t\ge1\). The function \(\psi\) is smooth, increasing, concave, \(\psi(0)=0\), and \(\psi(t)=1\) for \(t\ge1\), because \(2\int_0^1 s\sigma(s)\,ds=1\). Define
\[
v_j(z)=1-\sum_{i=1}^j\psi\bigl(j[-\log|P_i(z)|]\bigr),
\]
with the summand defined as \(1\) at a zero of \(P_i\). This convention agrees on a neighborhood of every zero since \(\psi\) is identically one for arguments at least one.

At any point of \(E\) at most one \(|P_i|\) is less than one. Two distinct reductions are coprime, so lifting a Bezout identity would otherwise express \(1\) as terms of absolute value strictly less than one, plus a polynomial of Gauss norm strictly less than one. This is impossible. Consequently \(0\le v_j\le1\). Each existing summand increases with \(j\), and the new summand is nonnegative, so \(v_{j+1}\le v_j\).

If all \(|P_i(z)|=1\), every nonzero polynomial has its Gauss norm at \(z\): normalize it and factor its reduction into the \(p_i\); the difference between that polynomial and a corresponding product of lifts has Gauss norm less than one. Thus \(z=\xi\), by equality of polynomial seminorms. For every other point there is one \(i\) with \(|P_i(z)|<1\); its summand is eventually exactly one. Hence \(v_j(z)\to0\) there, while \(v_j(\xi)=1\) for all \(j\).

To check smoothness and positivity, put \(H_j(s)=1-\psi(-js)\). It is smooth, convex, and nondecreasing on \(\mathbb R\), and equals zero for \(s\le-1/j\). Each \(H_j(\log|P_i|)\) is locally constant near the zero set and elsewhere is a smooth function of an invertible analytic logarithm. It is therefore ordinary CLD smooth, hence weakly smooth through that smooth harmonic tropicalization. Classical positivity follows from the supporting-line argument in 2.3, including zeros. Since
\[
v_j=\sum_{i=1}^jH_j(\log|P_i|)-(j-1),
\]
the cone and constant properties give classical positivity. Its local Hessian is a sum of terms
\[
H_j''(\log|P_i|)\,d'\log|P_i|\wedge d''\log|P_i|\ge0.
\]
On the analytic interior the associated strong and CLD currents are positive by 2.4. We make no boundary-free integration-by-parts claim on the entire closed disc. All expressions pull back under every analytic extension, remain weakly smooth and classically psh, remain decreasing, and converge to the pullback \(b\circ p\). The limit on an extension is that pullback, not a newly defined spike at the extension-field Gauss point alone.

**Higher-dimensional failure.** For any \(m\ge1\), put \(X=E\times\mathbb P^{m,\mathrm{an}}\) and \(u=b\circ\operatorname{pr}_E\). This is an arbitrary-class psh function on a separated good strict space of pure dimension \(m+1\), by GR pullback. Fix a \(K\)-rational projective point \(p\) and \(x=(\xi,p)\). The section \(s(z)=(z,p)\) pulls any alleged sequence on an ordinary neighborhood of \(x\) back to a sequence on an ordinary neighborhood of \(\xi\), which was proved impossible when the residue field is uncountable. Projection is proper and has no relative boundary. Boundary composition in GR 2.11, with the disc locally separated, gives
\[
\partial X=\operatorname{pr}_E^{-1}(\partial E).
\]
Thus \(x\) is an actual analytic boundary point. A concrete field is \(K=\mathbb C((t))\) with its complete nontrivial \(t\)-adic valuation. The obstruction survives passage to any analytic extension with uncountable residue field; the original failure needs no scalar descent argument.

## 6. Remaining original obligations and limits

Theorems A--C do not characterize all higher-dimensional good strict spaces with the P02.3 sheaf property. The positive higher-dimensional theorem is restricted to functions defined on saturated proper-fibre domains over the indicated singular curves. It does not settle nonsaturated affine germs, even on those products. The full boundaryless higher-dimensional arbitrary-class problem, a sharp general singularity range, nonperfect fields, arbitrary nonseparable fields for general singular targets, nonseparated spaces, and general analytic boundary configurations remain unresolved here. The exact residue-countability threshold proved in Section 5 concerns one concrete boundary family. It supplies a necessary exclusion for a universal sequential statement over all fields and spaces, not a sufficient geometric characterization. None of our assertions identifies weakly smooth with ordinary CLD smooth, or derives current positivity just from class membership. There is no assertion that a general regularization net has a countable cofinal subnet.

## 7. Reproducibility and evidence classification

The input is retained in input.json and byte-identical input-original.json; assignment-original.txt and task-original.json retain its selected fields. The complete proof is this file; P02.3-paper.tex embeds the same body as a standalone paper. source-extracts/source-provenance.json records SHA-256 checks, literal source-page excerpts including complete selected published proofs, exact premises used, and their application conditions. PDF pages 80--81 of GJR were rendered and visually inspected to resolve the decisive net/sequence and subdivision wording. The TeX diagnostics are in build-1.stdout.txt, build-2.stdout.txt, build-1.log, build-2.log, and build-diagnostics.json. The existing TeX installation is used without installation.

Rerun the retained source audit with `python3 collect_sources.py`, generate the standalone TeX with `python3 make_paper.py`, and build twice with `python3 build_paper.py`. `python3 audit_artifacts.py` checks byte preservation, required status keys, proof-body synchronization, and recorded build diagnostics. These are artifact checks, not proof checking. No computational experiment or exhaustive finite computation is presented as evidence for the mathematics. The mathematical claims are draft proofs relying on the explicitly listed published foundations. No heavy computation, external service, private campaign files, other model, or subagent was used.

    "detailedSummary": "Polynomial reductions show that every neighborhood of the Gauss point contains rational points in all but finitely many residue classes, producing an obstruction to any pointwise continuous sequence when the residue field is uncountable.     "text": "Draft boundary theorem. Let K be any complete nontrivially valued field, E=M(K<T>) the strict closed unit disc, and xi its radius-one Gauss point. Define b(xi)=1 and b(z)=0 otherwise. Then b is an upper semicontinuous classical psh function. If the residue field is uncountable, b is not the pointwise limit of any sequence of finite continuous functions on any ordinary neighborhood of xi, even without monotonicity. If the residue field is countable, the explicit construction below gives finite weakly smooth classical psh functions decreasing to b on the whole fixed E, with positive curvature on its analytic interior.\n\nClassical membership. The actual analytic boundary of E is {xi}. For 0<a≤1, {b<a}=E\\{xi} is open, so b is upper semicontinuous. It is constant zero on Int(E). GR Proposition 3.5(3) makes it subharmonic on E, and GR 4.1.2 makes it classically psh, including the required tests after every analytic extension. This is an analytic-boundary function, not an interior spike on a boundaryless space.\n\nNeighborhood lemma. Every ordinary neighborhood N of xi contains rational points from all but finitely many residue classes. Polynomial evaluations generate the spectrum topology because polynomials are uniformly dense in the Tate algebra. Choose a basic neighborhood given by finitely many polynomial absolute-value conditions. Normalize each nonzero polynomial by a coefficient of maximum absolute value; its reduction is a nonzero residue polynomial. It has only finitely many roots in the residue field. For a lift a∈K° of a residue class avoiding these roots, every selected polynomial has precisely its Gauss absolute value at a. Thus a lies in N.\n\nUncountable-residue obstruction. Suppose continuous q_j on N converge pointwise to b. Discard finitely many terms so q_j(xi)>3/4. Each {q_j>3/4}∩N is a neighborhood of xi and excludes rational points from only finitely many residue classes. An uncountable residue field has a class outside the countable union of these exceptions. Choose a lift a of that class. Then q_j(a)>3/4 for every remaining j, contradicting b(a)=0. Since weakly smooth functions are continuous, this excludes the requested sequential regularization. It does not exclude decreasing nets.\n\nCountable-residue construction. Enumerate the monic irreducible polynomials of the residue field as p_1,p_2,... and choose monic coefficientwise lifts P_i∈K°[T], each of Gauss norm one. Let sigma be a smooth nonnegative probability density supported in (0,1), symmetric about 1/2, and set\n  eta(t)=2 integral from t to 1 of sigma(s) ds,\n  psi(t)=integral from 0 to t of eta(s) ds,\nwith sigma zero outside (0,1). Then eta is smooth, nonnegative and nonincreasing, equals 2 for t≤0 and 0 for t≥1. Thus psi is smooth, increasing and concave, with psi(0)=0 and psi(t)=1 for t≥1; the latter follows from 2 integral_0^1 s sigma(s)ds=1.\n\nDefine\n  v_j(z)=1-sum_{i=1}^j psi(j[-log|P_i(z)|]),\nwith a summand defined to be 1 at a zero of P_i. This convention agrees on a whole neighborhood of each zero, because psi is constant for arguments at least 1.\n\nAt most one |P_i(z)| is less than one. Otherwise two coprime reductions admit a Bezout identity whose lift would express 1 as terms of absolute value strictly less than one plus an error polynomial of Gauss norm strictly less than one, contradicting the ultrametric inequality. Therefore 0≤v_j≤1. Existing summands increase with j and new summands are nonnegative, so v_{j+1}≤v_j.\n\nIf all |P_i(z)|=1, every polynomial has its Gauss norm at z. To see this, normalize the polynomial, factor its reduction into the p_i, and compare it with the corresponding product of lifts. Their difference has Gauss norm less than one. Consequently z=xi by equality of polynomial seminorms. Every other z has exactly one i with |P_i(z)|<1; its summand is eventually exactly 1. Hence v_j(z)→0 away from xi and v_j(xi)=1 throughout.\n\nSmoothness and positivity. Write H_j(s)=1-psi(-js). It is smooth, convex and nondecreasing on R, and zero for s≤-1/j. Each H_j(log|P_i|) is locally constant near the zero set and elsewhere a smooth function of an invertible analytic logarithm. It is therefore weakly smooth through an actual smooth harmonic tropicalization; ordinary CLD smoothness here follows from the explicit coordinates, not from a weak-smooth label. Classical positivity follows from the supporting-line argument in n2, including the constant supporting line at zeros. Since\n  v_j=sum_{i=1}^j H_j(log|P_i|)-(j-1),\nthe cone and constant properties prove classical positivity. Its local curvature is the sum of\n  H_j''(log|P_i|) d'log|P_i| wedge d''log|P_i|,\nwith nonnegative coefficients. The integration argument in n2 gives positive strong and CLD currents on Int(E). No boundary-free integration-by-parts assertion is made on the entire closed disc.\n\nAll formulas pull back after every analytic extension, remain weakly smooth and classically psh, remain decreasing, and converge to b∘p. That pullback can equal 1 at more than one extension-field point; it is not replaced by a new single-point Gauss spike.\n\nHigher-dimensional failure. For m≥1 take X=E×P^{m,an} and u=b∘pr_E. This is classical psh on a separated good strict pure-dimensional space of dimension m+1. Fix a K-rational projective point p and x=(xi,p). The analytic section s(z)=(z,p) pulls any alleged sequence on an ordinary neighborhood of x back to a continuous sequence on an ordinary neighborhood of xi, already proved impossible when the residue field is uncountable. Projection is proper with no relative boundary; boundary composition over the locally separated disc gives partial X=pr_E^{-1}({xi}). Thus x is an actual analytic boundary point. A concrete field is C((t)) with its complete nontrivial t-adic valuation. For an extension of this uncountable-residue example, the same obstruction uses the uncountable image of the original residue field. The countable-residue construction proves that countability is the exact threshold for this particular boundary-spike target; it is not a sufficient condition for all disc targets.\n\nRemaining original obligations. The positive higher-dimensional result n3 concerns saturated domains, not all nonsaturated affine sheaf germs. The full boundaryless higher-dimensional arbitrary-class problem, a sharp necessary-and-sufficient geometric characterization, general singularity and scalar-descent ranges, nonperfect fields, general nonseparable-field targets, nonseparated spaces and other analytic boundary configurations remain unresolved here. The obstruction is sequential and does not establish failure for nets. No known curve or invariant toric endpoint is identified with the original characterization.\n\nThe full proof and remaining obligations are retained in P02.3-argument.md, Sections 5–6, and P02.3-paper.tex. status.json has exactly id,status,exact_result,proof_file,concrete_gaps and marks P02.3 partial. python3 audit_artifacts.py reported all ten artifact checks true. Both final TeX passes returned zero and produced a 10-page PDF, with no diagnostics on the second pass. These are execution observations; the new proofs remain unverified drafts. Rerun python3 collect_sources.py; python3 make_paper.py; python3 build_paper.py; python3 audit_artifacts.py. The entire original target and its sharp general range have not been solved.\n\nArtifacts: /workspace/research/campaigns/round-43/P02.3-public-source-decreasing-weakly-smooth/artifacts/codex-4O8X0J",
    "support": [
      "n1",
      "n2"
    ]
  }

6                                    W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

2.7. Grothendieck topology and fibers. For a k-analytic space X , we will also consider
the G-topology generated by the analytic domains of X , as described in [Ber93, §1.3]. We
denote this space by X G to emphasize the use of this Grothendieck topology. For strictly
k-analytic spaces, we often use the G-topology generated by the strictly analytic domains
instead: see [Ber93, §1.6]. In either case, the space X G has a well-behaved sheaf of rings
OX G . If V = M (A ) is an affinoid domain of X , then we have OX G (V ) = A . For x ∈ X ,
we denote by H (x) the completed residue field of the local ring OX G ,x with respect to
the valuation on OX G ,x /m x induced by the norm corresponding to x. If V is any analytic
domain in X and if x ∈ V , then H (x) is the same whether we consider x as belonging
to V (which is itself a k-analytic space), or to X . We call x ∈ X a rig-point if H (x) is a
finite extension of k.
   If ϕ : Y → X is a morphism, then the fiber ϕ −1 (x) over x ∈ X is an H (x)-analytic
space, and the underlying topological space has the induced topology [Ber93, §1.4].

2.8. Fiber products. The category of k-analytic spaces admits fiber products by [Ber93,
§1.4]. For analytic spaces X , Y over a k-analytic space S, we denote the fiber product by
X ×S Y . Given x ∈ X and y ∈ Y over the same point s ∈ S, there always exists z ∈ X ×S Y
mapping to x and to y with respect to the canonical projections [Duc18, 1.2.13]. For
convenience, we repeat the argument. The completed tensor product H (x)⊗         b H (s) H ( y)
contains H (x) ⊗H (s) H ( y) by a result of Gruson [Gru66, §3.2 Théorème 1(4)], so
H (x)⊗b H (s) H ( y) ̸= 0. The Berkovich spectrum of any nonzero Banach ring is nonempty
                                                                 
[Ber90, Theorem 1.2.1], and any z ′ ∈ M H (x)⊗     b H (s) H ( y) induces a point z ∈ X ×S Y
mapping to x and y. Note that H (z) is a simultaneous analytic extension field of H (x)
and of H ( y).
  Since Gruson’s result holds for Banach algebras over a non-Archimedean field, the
same argument shows that for two analytic field extensions of k, there is a joint analytic
extension of both.

2.9. Extension of scalars. Let k′ /k be an an analytic extension field. Then there is a
base extension functor (·)k′ which associates to a k-analytic space X a k′ -analytic space
X k′ , given on a k-affinoid space X = M (A ) by the k′ -affinoid space X k′ = M (A ⊗b k k′ )
[Ber90, §1.4]. We denote by πk′ /k : X k′ → X the structure map, which is closed and
surjective by [GRW17, Lemma 2.19], [Duc24, (3.1.1.2)].

call σ the Shilov section. The Shilov section is continuous: see [Ber90, Lemma 3.2.2(i)],
[Duc18, 1.2.16].

2.11. Relative boundary. An important notion in this paper is that of the relative bound-
ary of a morphism ϕ : X → Y of k-analytic spaces. This is a closed subset of X which we
denote by ∂ (X /Y ). We will not give the details of this definition, but let us try to explain
the literature on the subject. In [Ber90, §2.5], Berkovich defines the relative boundary
∂ (X /Y ) for k-affinoid spaces X and Y . Then in [Ber90, §3.1], he generalizes ∂ (X /Y ) for
good k-analytic spaces using that all points of X and Y have k-affinoid neighborhoods.
Berkovich calls ϕ closed if ∂ (X /Y ) = ;. Berkovich defines ∂ (X /Y ) for arbitrary k-analytic
spaces in [Ber93, 1.5.3(ii)], as follows. A morphism ϕ is closed if for every morphism
Y ′ → Y from a good k-analytic space Y ′ , the fiber product X ′ := X ×Y Y ′ is good and the
canonical morphism X ′ → Y ′ is closed in the previous sense. Finally in [Ber93, Defini-
tion 1.5.4], a point x ∈ X is defined to be in Int(X /Y ) if x has an open neighborhood U
such that ϕ induces a closed morphism U → Y in the above sense. We refer to [Tem15,
Definition 4.2.4.1] for a more explicit equivalent definition of ∂ (X /Y ). Note that in this
paper, we will use the notion closed map in the usual topological sense meaning that it
maps closed sets to closed sets. This is different from Berkovich’s terminology.
   The complement Int(X /Y ) := X \ ∂ (X /Y ) is called the relative interior. We will usually
apply this in the case Y = M (k), in which case we call ∂ X := ∂ (X /M (k)) the boundary
of X and Int(X ) := X \ ∂ X the interior. If ∂ X = ;, we say that X boundaryless. A bound-
aryless space is good by definition. Note that Berkovich calls boundaryless k-analytic
spaces closed.
   We list here some properties of the boundary and interior which we will use throughout
the paper:
   (1) A point x ∈ X is contained in Int(X /Y ) if and only if there is an open neighbor-
       hood U of x such that Int(U/Y ) = U: this is part of the above definition.
   (2) If ϕ : X → Y is finite, then Int(X /Y ) = X [Ber93, Example 1.5.3(iii)].
   (3) If X is an analytic domain in Y , then Int(X /Y ) is the topological interior of X in
       Y [Ber93, Proposition 1.5.5(i)].

2.13. A k-analytic space X is called rig-smooth at x ∈ X if it is flat (in the sense of
[Duc18]) and if the sheaf of Kähler differentials ΩX (considered on the G-topology) is
free at x of rank equal to the dimension of X at y. We call X smooth if it is rig-smooth
and boundaryless. We refer to [Duc18, Chapter 5] for a discussion (note that rig-smooth
is called quasi-smooth there) and comparison to other definitions.

                   3. SUBHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON ANALYTIC CURVES
  In this section, we generalize Thuillier’s theory [Thu05] of subharmonic functions from
smooth strictly analytic curves to arbitrary k-analytic curves.
3.1 (k-Analytic Curves). By a k-analytic curve we mean a separated k-analytic space of
pure dimension 1. We gather the following facts about k-analytic curves:
   (1) A k-analytic curve is good, i.e., every point has an affinoid neighborhood. [Duc24,
        Proposition 3.3.7]
   (2) A k-analytic curve is paracompact. [Duc24, Théorème 4.5.10]
   (3) If X is an affinoid k-analytic curve, then ∂ X coincides with the Shilov boundary
        of X . [Thu05, 2.1.2]
An analytic domain in a k-analytic curve (for example, an open subset) is again a k-
analytic curve.
3.2 (Thuillier’s Harmonic Functions). Thuillier [Thu05, §2.3] defines a sheaf of harmonic
functions on a smooth, strictly k-analytic curve for a non-trivially valued k, roughly as
follows. If X is affinoid, then a continuous function h: X → R is harmonic if, after passing
to a finite, separable field extension, it coincides with the composition of a harmonic
function on a skeleton of X (in the sense of graph theory) with the retraction to the
skeleton. If X is smooth, then a harmonic function on an open subset of X is defined to be
a continuous function whose restriction to every strictly affinoid subdomain is harmonic.
3.3 (Thuillier’s Subharmonic Functions). For a non-trivally valued k, Thuillier [Thu05,
Définition 3.1.5] defines a subharmonic function on a smooth, strictly k-analytic curve X
to be an upper semicontinuous function u: X → R ∪ {−∞} that is not identically −∞
on any connected component of X and that satisfies the following property: for every
strictly k-affinoid domain U ⊂ X and every harmonic function h: U → R, if u ≤ h on ∂ U
then u ≤ h on U.
  Now we extend this definition to the case of non-smooth curves. The idea is that a
function should be subharmonic if it pulls back to a subharmonic function along any
morphism from a smooth curve. However, an inseparable curve over an imperfect field
does not admit any non-constant morphisms from a smooth curve, so one has to allow
an extension of scalars as well. We also consider the constant function −∞ to be sub-
harmonic, following Demailly [Dem85] in the complex case.
Definition 3.4 (Subharmonic functions). Let X be a k-analytic curve and let u: X →
R ∪ {−∞} be an upper semicontinuous function. We say that u is subharmonic provided
that, for every non-trivially valued analytic extension field k′ /k, every smooth, connected,
strictly k′ -analytic curve Y , and every morphism Y → X k′ , the composition
                                        πk′ /k     u
                              Y −→ X k′ −→ X −→ R ∪ {−∞}
             CLASSICALLY PSH AND PLURIHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON BERKOVICH SPACES              11

is either subharmonic on Y in the sense of [Thu05, Définition 3.1.5], or is identically −∞.
3.4.1. If X is a smooth, connected, strictly k-analytic curve over a non-trivially valued k,
then an upper semicontinuous function u: X → R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic in our sense
if and only if it is either subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense or is identically −∞. Indeed, if
u is subharmonic in our sense then one can take k = k′ and Y = X to see that it is either
subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense or is identically −∞. Conversely, if u is identically −∞
then it is clearly subharmonic in our sense, and if u is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense
then it is subharmonic in our sense by [Thu05, Proposition 3.1.14 and Corollaire 3.4.5].
3.4.2. As a special case of (3.4.1), if X is a smooth, connected, strictly k-analytic curve
over a non-trivially valued k, then a function u: X → R (taking finite values) is subhar-
monic in our sense if and only if it is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense.
  Here we collect the basic properties of subharmonic functions, which we will prove
over the course of this section.
Proposition 3.5. Let X be a k-analytic curve and let u: X → R ∪ {−∞} be an upper
semicontinuous function.
   (1) (Sheafiness) The subharmonic functions form a sheaf on X .
   (2) (Analytic functions) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX ) then log | f | : X → R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic.
   (3) (Boundaries) If u is subharmonic on Int(X ), then u is subharmonic on X .
   (4) (Cone Property) If u1 , u2 : X → R ∪ {−∞} are subharmonic and λ1 , λ2 ∈ R≥0 then
       λ1 u1 + λ2 u2 and max{u1 , u2 } are subharmonic.
   (5) (Limits) The infimum of a decreasing net of subharmonic functions is subharmonic.
   (6) (Maximum Principle) If u is subharmonic and u attains a local maximum at x ∈
       Int(X ), then u is constant in a neighborhood of x.
   (7) (Extension of scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. Then u is subharmonic
       if and only if u ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R ∪ {∞} is subharmonic.
   (8) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic curve and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If u
       is subharmonic then u ◦ f is subharmonic, and the converse holds if f is finite and
       surjective.
   (9) (Finite Values) If u is subharmonic and u(x) = −∞ for a non-rig-point x ∈ X ,
       then u ≡ −∞ on the irreducible component of Int(X ) ∪ {x} containing x.
  Let us make some remarks regarding the content of Proposition 3.5.
3.5.1. If U ⊂ X is an open subset then U is again a k-analytic curve, so it makes sense
to speak of subharmonic functions on U. It is immediate from the definition that a sub-
harmonic function on X restricts to a subharmonic function on U. Hence subharmonic
functions form a presheaf on X . Proposition 3.5(1) asserts that this presheaf is a sheaf.
3.5.2. The maximum principle (Proposition 3.5(6)) is [Thu05, Proposition 3.4.10] in the
smooth, strictly analytic case.
3.5.3. In the situation of Proposition 3.5(7), it is immediate from Definition 3.4 that
u ◦ πk′ /k is subharmonic when u is subharmonic. This is the difficult direction in the
smooth, strictly analytic case: see [Thu05, Corollaire 3.4.5]. This result is hidden in the
12                                 W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

fact that Definition 3.4 essentially recovers Thuillier’s definition of subharmonicity in that
case 3.4.1.
3.5.4. In the situation of Proposition 3.5(8), it is immediate from Definition 3.4 that u◦ f
is subharmonic when u is subharmonic. The remark in 3.5.3 applies to a lesser extent in
this case: see [Thu05, Proposition 3.1.14].
3.5.5. For any x ∈ X , the subset Int(X )∪{x} is open in X . This is obvious if x ∈ Int(X ), and
if x ∈ ∂ X then it follows from the fact that ∂ X is discrete in X . In particular, Int(X ) ∪ {x}
is again a k-analytic curve, so it makes sense to speak of its irreducible components
in Proposition 3.5(9). Irreducible components can only meet at rig-points (their inter-
sections are Zariski-closed), so a non-rig-point x is contained in a unique irreducible
component of Int(X ) ∪ {x}; this is implicit in Proposition 3.5(9).
3.5.6. Proposition 3.5(9) implies that if u is subharmonic and u(x) = −∞ for a non-rig-
point x ∈ X , then u ≡ −∞ in a neighborhood of x.
3.6. In order to extend Thuillier’s results, we will pass from a k-analytic curve to a
smooth, strictly k-analytic curve in four steps:
      (1) Extend scalars to a field as in 2.10 to make the curve strictly analytic.
                                                                                          −∞
      (2) If char(k) = p, then extend scalars to the completion of the perfect closure k p .
      (3) Replace the analytic curve by the underlying reduced curve.
      (4) Pass to the interior of the normalization, which is smooth (see 2.13).
We begin with some lemmas about this process.
                                                                                 −∞
Lemma 3.7. Suppose that char(k) = p > 0. Let k′ be the completion of k p . Then k′ is
perfect, and if X is a k-analytic space then πk′ /k : X k′ → X is a homeomorphism such that
π(∂ X k′ ) = ∂ X .
Proof. We leave it as an exercise for the reader to verify that k′ is perfect. The fact that
πk′ /k is a homeomorphism is [Duc09, Remarque 0.5], and the assertion about boundaries
is Lemma 2.12.                                                                            □
     We make the following observation to mirror Lemma 3.7. Its proof is immediate.
Lemma 3.8. Let X be a strictly k-analytic space and let ι : X red ,→ X be the inclusion of the
underlying reduced space. Then ι is a homeomorphism of underlying topological spaces, and
ι(∂ X red ) = ∂ X .
  We refer the reader to [Ber90, before Proposition 3.1.8] for the construction of the

Proof of Proposition 3.5(1). This follows from the corresponding fact for subharmonic
functions on smooth, strictly analytic curves [Thu05, Corollaire 3.1.13], except that one
has to be careful about where a function takes the value −∞. Suppose then that {Ui }
is an open cover of X and that u: X → R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic on each Ui . Note that
u is upper semicontinuous. Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field with k′ non-trivially
valued, let Y be a smooth, connected, strictly k′ -analytic curve, and let f : Y → X k′ be a
morphism. Assume that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ̸≡ −∞, so we must show that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f is sub-
harmonic in Thuillier’s sense. Let Ui′ = π−1 k′ /k

is subharmonic on Ui , the composition u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f |Yi is either subharmonic in Thuillier’s
sense or is identically −∞ on each connected component of Yi .
   Let W1 be the set of all points y ∈ Y that admit a neighborhood on which u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f
is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense, and let W2 be the set of all points y ∈ Y that admit a
neighborhood on which u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ≡ −∞. Clearly W1 and W2 are open, and they cover
Y since Y is covered by the connected components of the Yi . They are disjoint because if
u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic in Thuillier’s sense on a connected neighborhood of y ∈ Y ,
then it cannot be identically −∞ on any neighborhood of y [Thu05, Lemme 3.1.9].
Since Y is connected and u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ̸≡ −∞, we have Y = W1 . The result now follows
from [Thu05, Corollaire 3.1.13].                                                                  □


Proof of Proposition 3.5(2). Let f be an analytic function on X . The function | f | is con-
tinuous and hence u := log | f | is upper semicontinuous as a continuous function with
values in R ∪ {−∞}. According to our definition of subharmonic functions, we have to
show that after base change of u with respect to an analytic field extension k′ /k with k′
non-trivially valued and then pull-back to a smooth strictly k′ -analytic curve, the resulting
function is subharmonic. This boils down to showing that u is subharmonic in the special
case of a smooth strictly k-analytic curve X . For this, we may assume X connected. If
f = 0, then u is identically −∞ and hence subharmonic in the sense of Definition 3.4. If
f is not identically zero, then the Poincaré–Lelong formula [Thu05, Proposition 3.3.15]
and the characterization in terms of positive currents [Thu05, Proposition 3.4.4] show
that u is subharmonic in the sense of Thuillier.                                            □

x is not a rig-point of X , there exists a unique point x ′ ∈ Xe ′ lying over x by Lemmas 3.7,
3.8 and 3.9. We must show that u◦π◦ι ◦ν ≡ −∞ on Xe ′ . Since u◦π◦ι ◦ν is subharmonic
by Proposition 3.5(7,8), we may replace X by Xe ′ to assume that X is rig-smooth and
connected (and still that ∂ X = ; or ∂ X = {x} using the quoted lemmas again).
    If ∂ X = ; then X is smooth and connected, so u is either identically −∞ or it is sub-
harmonic in Thuillier’s sense. The latter case is impossible when u(x) = −∞ by [Thu05,
Proposition 3.4.10].
    Suppose then that X is rig-smooth and connected, and that ∂ X = {x} and u(x) = −∞.
By Lemma 3.11, there is an open neighborhood U of x on which u ≡ −∞. Since X
is connected, every connected component of Int(X ) contains x in its closure. Thus U
intersects every connected component C of Int(X ). This implies that u takes the value
−∞ on a non-rig point of C, so the previous paragraph shows that u ≡ −∞ on C. It
follows that u ≡ −∞ on X .                                                                           □
3.12. Now we turn to harmonic functions. Let X be a smooth, strictly k-analytic curve
with k non-trivially valued and let h: X → R be a function. We claim that h is harmonic
in the sense of (3.2) if and only if h and −h are subharmonic (in our sense or Thuillier’s—
see (3.4.2)). This is implicit in Thuillier’s thesis, but it requires justification. Suppose then
that h: X → R is harmonic. Then ±h are subharmonic by [Thu05, Proposition 2.3.13 and
Corollaire 3.1.12]. Conversely, suppose that ±h are subharmonic. This implies that h is
continuous. Let U ⊂ X be a strictly affinoid domain. By [Thu05, Lemme 2.3.9], there
exists a unique harmonic function h′ : U → R that coincides with h on ∂ U. By definition
20                                 W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

of subharmonicity, we have h ≤ h′ on U. By the same argument as applied to −h, we see
that h = h′ on U, so that h is harmonic on U. As U was arbitrary, h is harmonic.
  By 3.12, the following definition recovers Thuillier’s definition of harmonicity on a
smooth, strictly k-analytic curve, and even on a strictly affinoid rig-smooth curve us-
ing [Thu05, Corollaire 2.3.14].
Definition 3.13 (Harmonic functions). Let X be a k-analytic curve for a possibly trivially
valued non-Archimedean field k. A function h: X → R is harmonic if h and −h are both
subharmonic.
   The advantage of this definition is that the following basic properties of harmonic func-
tions follow immediately from the corresponding properties of subharmonic functions.
Proposition 3.14. Let X be a k-analytic curve and let h: X → R be a continuous function.
     (1) (Sheafiness) The harmonic functions form a sheaf on X .
     (2) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX× ) then log | f | is harmonic.
     (3) The harmonic functions on X form a real vector space.
     (4) (Limits) If h is locally a uniform limit of harmonic functions, then it is harmonic.
     (5) (Maximum Principle) If h is harmonic and h attains a local extremum at x ∈ Int(X ),
         then h is constant in a neighborhood of x.
     (6) (Extension of Scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. Then h is harmonic
         if and only if h ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R is harmonic.
     (7) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic curve and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If h is
         harmonic then h ◦ f is harmonic, and the converse holds if f is finite and surjective.
     (8) (Boundaries) If h is harmonic on Int(X ), then it is harmonic on X .
   We will later see in Theorem 7.6 that (4) holds even for pointwise limits. The fol-
lowing proposition asserts that Definition 3.13 is equivalent to the notion of harmonicity
in [GJR21, Definition 7.2]. We assume that k is non-trivially valued as in ibid.
Proposition 3.15. Let X be a strictly k-analytic curve and let h: X → R be a continuous
function. The function h is harmonic if and only if it is R-PL and the line bundle Lh (x) ∈
Pic(red(X , x))R defined in (2.17) is numerically trivial for every point x ∈ X .
Proof. This is [GJR21, Proposition 15.7] in the rig-smooth case; we reduce to this case
                                                                         −∞
in the usual way. If char(k) = p > 0 let k′ be the completion of k p , and otherwise let
k′ = k. Let X ′ = X ⊗b k k′ and let π: X ′ → X be the structure morphism. Let ι : X red
                                                                                      ′
                                                                                        ,→ X ′
be the underlying reduced space and let ν: Xe ′ → X red  ′
                                                            be the normalization, so that Xe ′ is
rig-smooth. Let f : Xe ′ → X denote the composition π ◦ ι ◦ ν.
   Suppose that h is harmonic. Then h ◦ f is harmonic by Proposition 3.14(6,7), so h ◦ f
is R-PL and Lh (x ′ ) is numerically trivial for all x ′ ∈ Xe ′ by the rig-smooth case. Then
h is R-PL by Lemma 3.9(6) and [GJR21, Lemma 5.3(3), Corollary 5.11], and Lh (x) is
numerically trivial for all x ∈ X by [GJR21, Proposition 7.6(1′ , 2′ )]. Conversely, suppose
that h is R-PL and that Lh (x) is numerically trivial for all x ∈ X . The same holds for
h ◦ f by [GJR21, Proposition 7.6(1′ , 2′ )], so h ◦ f is harmonic by the rig-smooth case, and
hence h is harmonic by Proposition 3.14(6,7).                                                  □

Remark 3.16. We still assume that X is a strictly k-analytic curve over a non-trivially
valued field k and that h: X → R is continuous. Proposition 3.15 and Proposition 3.14(6)
imply in particular that if h◦πk′ /k is harmonic for some analytic extension field k′ /k, then
h◦πk′′ /k is R-PL for any analytic extension k′′ /k, which is by no means obvious. Likewise,
Proposition 3.14(7) implies that if f : Y → X is finite and surjective and h◦ f is harmonic,
then h is R-PL, and Proposition 3.14(8) implies that if h|Int(X ) is harmonic then h is R-PL.

                     4. CLASSICALLY PLURISUBHARMONIC FUNCTIONS
  In this section we define the notion of a classically plurisubharmonic function. The
definition follows Definition 3.4.
Definition 4.1 (Classically Plurisubharmonic Functions). Let X be a k-analytic space and
let u: X → R ∪ {−∞} be an upper semicontinuous function. We say that u is classically
plurisubharmonic or classically psh provided that, for every analytic extension field k′ /k,
every k′ -analytic curve Y , and every morphism f : Y → X k′ , the function u◦πk′ /k ◦ f : Y →
R ∪ {−∞} is subharmonic on Y in the sense of Definition 3.4.
4.1.1. Any class of psh functions should respect extension of scalars and should be stable
under pullback, so the conditions in Definition 4.1 are necessary. In complex analysis,
this condition (without the extension of scalars) is the definition of plurisubharmonicity,
which is why we call the notion “classically psh”.
4.1.2. If X is a k-analytic curve, then a function u: X → R∪{−∞} is classically psh if and
only if it is subharmonic. Indeed, if u is classically psh then taking k′ = k and Y = X in
Definition 4.1 shows that u is subharmonic, and if u is subharmonic then it is classically
psh by Proposition 3.5(7,8).
4.1.3. In Definition 4.1, one can assume that Y is a connected, smooth, strictly k′ -analytic
curve: this follows from Definition 3.4.
  Classically psh functions satisfy the basic properties of subharmonic functions on curves.
Proposition 4.2. Let X be a k-analytic space.
   (1) (Sheafiness) The classically psh functions form a sheaf on X .
   (2) If f ∈ Γ (X , OX ) then log | f |: X → R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh.
   (3) (Cone Property) If u1 , u2 : X → R∪{−∞} are classically psh and λ1 , λ2 ∈ R≥0 then
       λ1 u1 + λ2 u2 and max{u1 , u2 } are classically psh.
   (4) (Limits) The infimum of a decreasing net of classically psh functions is classically
       psh. For arbitrary pointwise limits, we refer to Theorem 6.5.
   (5) (Extension of Scalars) Let k′ /k be an analytic extension field. A function u: X →
       R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh if and only if u ◦ πk′ /k : X k′ → R ∪ {−∞} is classically
       psh.
   (6) (Functoriality) Let Y be a k-analytic space and let f : Y → X be a morphism. If
       u: X → R ∪ {−∞} is classically psh then u ◦ f is classically psh, and the converse
       holds if f is finite and surjective.
Proof. Properties (1,3,4) follow immediately from Proposition 3.5(1,4,5). For Proposi-
tion 4.2(2), we consider an analytic function f on X . As | f | is continuous, we deduce
22                                  W. GUBLER AND J. RABINOFF

that log | f | is a continuous function with values in R ∪ {−∞} and hence it is upper semi-
continuous. Then Proposition 4.2(2) follows from Proposition 3.5(2).
    To prove Proposition 4.2(5), it is clear from the definitions that if u is classically psh,
then u ◦ πk′ /k is classically psh for every analytic extension field k′ /k. To prove the con-
verse, we assume that k′ /k is an analytic extension field and that u′ := u ◦ πk′ /k is a
classically psh function on X k′ . Since the morphism πk′ /k is closed and surjective 2.9,
it is clear that u is upper semicontinuous. Let k′′ /k be an analytic extension field and
let f : Y → X k′′ be a morphism over k′′ from a k′′ -analytic curve Y . We have to show
that u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic. As in the proof of Proposition 3.5(7), we consider
a simultaneous analytic extension field K of k′ and k′′ . It follows from the above that
u ◦ πK/k = u′ ◦ πK/k′ is classically psh and hence u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K is subharmonic. We con-
clude from the commutative diagram (3.10.1) that u ◦ πK/k ◦ f K = u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f ◦ πK/k′′ is
subharmonic and hence u ◦ πk′′ /k ◦ f is subharmonic by Proposition 3.5(7).
    Likewise, the proof of Proposition 4.2(6) follows the same lines as the proof of Propo-
sition 3.5(8). Again, it follows from the definitions if u is a classically psh function on X ,
then u ◦ f is a classically psh function on Y . Conversely, we assume that u ◦ f is a clas-
sically psh function for a finite surjective morphism f : Y → X . Since finite morphisms
are proper and hence closed, we deduce from upper semicontinuity of u ◦ f and from
surjectivity of f that u is upper semicontinuous. It remains to show that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g is
subharmonic for any analytic extension field k′ /k and any morphism g : Z → X k′ from a
connected k′ -analytic curve Z. Setting W := Z ×X k′ Yk′ , we get a morphism g ′ : W → Yk′
from f by base change with respect to f ′ : Yk′ → X k′ as in (3.10.2). Since u ◦ f is classi-
cally psh, it follows from Proposition 4.2(5) that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ′ = u ◦ f ◦ πk′ /k is classically
psh. The morphism f is finite and surjective, hence the same holds for f ′ . We con-
clude that the base change f ′′ : W → Z of the morphism f ′ is finite and surjective. The
k′ -analytic space W has irreducible components of dimension 1 and maybe isolated irre-
ducible components of dimension 0. Removing the latter, we obtain a k′ -analytic curve
W ′ as an open and closed subset of W . Since Z is a connected k′ -analytic curve and f ′′
is a finite surjective map from W onto Z, we get an induced finite surjective morphism
W ′ → Z. Indeed, as W ′ is also Zariski-closed in W , we deduce from finiteness of f ′′ that
f ′′ (W ′ ) is a Zariski-closed subset in Z of dimension 1 and hence f ′′ (W ′ ) = Z as Z is a
connected k′ -analytic curve. Since the inclusion W ′ → W is obviously finite, the com-
position W ′ → Z is also finite. In the following, we replace W by W ′ , which preserves
the commutativity of the diagram (3.10.2) and has the advantage that W is a k′ -analytic
curve. It follows that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ f ′ ◦ g ′ = u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g ◦ f ′′ is subharmonic on W . The
morphism of k′ -analytic curves f ′′ : W → Z is finite and surjective, so we deduce from
Proposition 3.5(8) that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ g is subharmonic.                                           □

  In the paper [GR25], we have studied semipositive R-PL functions on a good strictly
k-analytic space over a non-trivially valued non-archimedean field k. This turns out to
be the same as an R-PL function that is classically psh, as we now show.

Theorem 4.3. Suppose that the valuation on k is nontrivial. Let X be a good, strictly k-
analytic space and let h: X → R be an R-PL function. Then h is semipositive if and only if h
is classically psh.
              CLASSICALLY PSH AND PLURIHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON BERKOVICH SPACES                  23

Proof. For x ∈ X , an R-PL function h: X → R induces a residue line bundle Lh (x) ∈
Pic(red(X , x))R on the reduction red(X , x) of the germ (X , x): see 2.17. Recall from
[GJR21, Definition 7.2] that h is semipositive at x ∈ X if and only if the associated residue
line bundle Lh (x) is nef. We have shown in [GR25, Proposition 3.1] that for a smooth
strictly k-analytic curve, an R-PL function is semipositive if and only if it is subharmonic.
Since semipositivity is stable under base extension and pull-back [GJR21, Proposition
7.6], we conclude that a semipositive R-PL function on X is classically psh.
   The converse follows from [GR25, Corollary 3.5], which shows that an R-PL function h
on X is semipositive if and only if there is an algebraically closed analytic extension field
F of k such that for every smooth F -analytic curve C and every morphism ϕ : C → X F ,
we have h ◦ ϕ semipositive.                                                                □
                                 5. CONNECTIVITY BY CURVES
  We will prove a maximum principle for classically psh functions by reducing to the
case of curves. For this we will need to prove that it is possible to connect a point of an
analytic space to its neighbors using analytic curves, potentially defined over large field
extensions.
Definition 5.1. Let X be a k-analytic space. An analytic curve in X is a subset Γ ⊂ X
satisfying the following property: there is an analytic extension field k′ /k, a compact,
separated k′ -analytic space C of dimension at most 1, and a morphism ϕ : C → X k′ , such
that Γ = πk′ /k ◦ ϕ(C).
  An analytic curve Γ in X is overconvergent if there exist (k′ , C, ϕ) as above such that ϕ
factors as

                                                           →
                                               X k′
where C ′ is a separated k′ -analytic space of dimension at most 1, and ϕ ′ and ψ are
morphisms.
5.1.2. The compactness hypothesis in Definition 5.1 is crucial. When X is Hausdorff, it
implies that if Γ , Γ ′ are analytic curves in X such that Γ ∪ Γ ′ is connected, then Γ ∩ Γ ′ ̸= ;
(the compact sets Γ , Γ ′ are closed, so they cannot be disjoint by connectedness). However,
a non-proper compact curve has a nonempty boundary, which causes problems when
trying to reduce the maximum principle to the case of curves. This is the reason for
introducing overconvergent curves in X .
5.1.3. One can always pass to a larger analytic extension field k′′ /k′ in Definition 5.1.
Indeed, we have a commutative diagram

after recentering, we can assume that ϕ(V ) ⊂ B+n . For x ′ ∈ V let y ′ = ϕ(x ′ ) ∈ B+n and
let Σ x ′ be the connected component of ϕ −1 ([ y ′ , 0]) containing x ′ . Since [ y ′ , 0] ⊂ B+n we
have ϕ −1 ([ y ′ , 0]) ⊂ Int(Y ), and since Σ x ′ is connected and x ′ ∈ V we have Σ x ′ ⊂ V . As
before, there exists an open neighborhood U of x in V such that Σ x ′ ∩ Σ x ̸= ; for all
x ′ ∈ U.
     By Lemma 5.9, for any y ′ ∈ B+n there is a connected, overconvergent analytic curve
Γ y ′ in B+n containing [ y ′ , 0]. For x ′ ∈ V mapping to y ′ , we let Γ x ′ be the connected
component of ϕ −1 (Γ y ′ ) containing x ′ , so that Γ x ′ ⊂ V for the same reason that Σ x ′ ⊂ V .
By Lemma 5.2(1,4), this Γ x ′ is again a connected, overconvergent analytic curve in X .
Clearly Σ x ′ ⊂ Γ x ′ , so for x ′ ∈ U we have Γ x ′ ∩ Γ x ̸= ;. This completes the proof as in the
previous case.                                                                                     □

              6. THE MAXIMUM PRINCIPLES FOR CLASSICALLY PSH FUNCTIONS
  In this section, we consider a non-Archimedean field k. We will prove first that the
local maximum principle holds for classically psh functions, and then we will show a
global maximum principle in the affinoid case; more specifically, that that a classically
psh function takes its global maximum at a Shilov point.
Theorem 6.1 (Local Maximum Principle). Let X be a k-analytic space and let u be a clas-
sically psh function on X . If u has a local maximum at x ∈ Int(X ), then u is locally constant
at x.
Proof. We may replace X by a connected, Hausdorff open neighborhood of x in Int(X ) to
assume that X is connected and boundaryless and that u attains a global maximum at x.
Let R = u(x). Let Γ be a connected, overconvergent analytic curve in X containing x. We
claim that u ≡ R on Γ . Consider the set Γu=R = {z ∈ Γ | u(z) = R}. This set is closed in Γ
by upper semicontinuity of u, so since Γ is connected, it suffices to show that Γu=R is open
in Γ .
   By 5.1.3, there exist an analytic extension field k′ /k, a compact, separated strictly k′ -
analytic space C of dimension at most 1, a separated strictly k′ -analytic space C ′ of dimen-
sion at most 1, and morphisms ψ: C → C ′ and ϕ ′ : C ′ → X k′ , such that ψ(C) ⊂ Int(C ′ )
and πk′ /k ◦ ϕ(C) = Γ , where ϕ = ϕ ′ ◦ ψ. The composition u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ ′ is subharmonic
on 1-dimensional connected components of C ′ because u is classically psh.
   Let y ∈ Γu=R , let z ∈ C be a point lying over y, and let z ′ = ψ(z) ∈ Int(C ′ ), so that
R = u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ ′ (z ′ ) = u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ(z). If {z ′ } is a connected component of C ′ then clearly
u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ ′ ≡ R in a neighborhood of z ′ . Otherwise, the connected component of z ′
in C ′ is a strictly k′ -analytic curve, in which case u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ ′ ≡ R in a neighborhood
of z ′ by Proposition 3.5(6) (here we use z ′ ∈ Int(C ′ )). Taking the ψ-inverse image of
such a neighborhood, we see that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ ≡ R in a neighborhood of z. Applying
this argument to each z ∈ (πk′ /k ◦ ϕ)−1 ( y), we construct an open set V ⊂ C containing
(πk′ /k ◦ ϕ)−1 ( y) such that u ◦ πk′ /k ◦ ϕ|V ≡ R.
   The complement C \ V is a closed subset of the compact space C, so it is a compact
subset of C that is disjoint from (πk′ /k ◦ ϕ)−1 ( y). Its image is a compact subset of X that
does not meet y, so there is an open neighborhood W of y such that (πk′ /k ◦ϕ)−1 (W ) ⊂ V .
              CLASSICALLY PSH AND PLURIHARMONIC FUNCTIONS ON BERKOVICH SPACES                     31

It follows that u ≡ R on W ∩ Γ . This proves that Γu=R is open in Γ . As remarked above,
this yields u ≡ R on Γ .
   Let y ∈ X be any point. By Theorem 5.4, there is a connected, overconvergent analytic
curve Γ in X containing x and y. We showed above that u ≡ R on Γ , so that u( y) = R.
As y was arbitrary, we have u ≡ R on X .                                              □

  The global maximum principle for classically psh functions involves the Shilov bound-
ary. For this, we need the following two lemmas. The first is well-known, but we were
unable to find a reference.

Lemma 6.2. Let X be a k-affinoid space of pure dimension d ≥ 1. Then the Shilov boundary
of X is contained in the relative boundary ∂ X of X over k. In particular, ∂ X ̸= ;.

Proof. Let k′ /k be an analytic field extension such that X k′ is strictly affinoid. Note that
the base change morphism πk′ /k maps ∂ X k′ to ∂ X 2.11(5′ ) and maps the Shilov boundary
onto the Shilov boundary [GRW17, Proposition 2.21], so we may assume that X is strictly
affinoid and k′ non-trivially valued. Choose a formal affine k◦ -model X of X . The interior
X \ ∂ X consists of the points x ∈ X such that the closure of redX (x) in Xs is proper over
the residue field ek by [CD12, Lemme 6.5.1]. Since X is affine, the special fiber Xs is
affine, so x ∈ ∂ X if and only if redX (x) is not a closed point of Xs . Since d ≥ 1, the
special fiber Xs is of pure dimension d ≥ 1, and hence the generic points of Xs are not
closed. By [GM19, Proposition A.3], the divisorial points of X (i.e. the points of X which
reduce to the generic points of Xs ) are precisely the Shilov points of X . We conclude that
all Shilov points are in the boundary of X .                                                □

   Let X = M (A ) be an affinoid space over k of pure dimension d. By a result of Ducros
[Duc12, Lemme 3.1], the boundary ∂ X of X can be expressed as a union of affinoid spaces
of lower dimension, defined over certain extension fields of k. We use his argument to
give a stratification of ∂ X . Choose non-nilpotent elements g1 , . . . , g r ∈ A such that the
graded reductions e   g1• , . . . , e
